As illustrated in Figure 1, the overall workflow systematically progresses through four interconnected stages: first, coupling spatial topological distances and hydraulic head similarities to divide the network into physically meaningful operational zones; second, simulating diverse leakage scenarios via Monte Carlo sampling on virtual mid-pipe nodes during nighttime low-demand conditions and applying Pearson correlation alongside ANOVA feature selection to identify an optimal, non-redundant sensor subset; third, benchmarking heterogeneous base learners (RF, XGBoost, and MLP) against an independent LightGBM baseline, and fusing their complementary mechanisms using Voting and Stacking ensemble architectures (with a Logistic Regression meta-learner); and finally, evaluating localization accuracy and robustness under measurement noise perturbations while employing KernelExplainer SHAP analysis and confusion matrix cross-examination to uncover the underlying hydraulic logic governing model predictions.\n\n  * Article\n\n  * Image: Open Access\n\n7 September 2026\n\n28 Pages\n\n# An Interpretable Ensemble Learning Framework for Leakage Localization in Urban Water Distribution Networks\n\nQingfu Li and\n\nAo Chen^{ *}\n\nSchool of Water Conservancy and Transportation, Zhengzhou University, Zhengzhou 450001, China\n\n^{*}\n\nAuthor to whom correspondence should be addressed.\n\nWater2026, 18(17), 2220;https://doi.org/10.3390/w18172220\n\nThis article belongs to the Section Urban Water Management\n\nVersion Notes\n\nOrder Reprints\n\n## Abstract\n\nRapid and accurate leakage localization in urban water distribution networks (WDNs) is vital for reducing non-revenue water and supporting sustainable water resource management. This study develops a spatial–hydraulic coupled and interpretable machine learning framework for zonal leakage localization under operational uncertainties. The WDN is first partitioned into zones, followed by leakage-region prediction using a Stacking ensemble integrating Random Forest, XGBoost, and MLP. SHAP analysis is employed to characterize the contribution of individual monitoring nodes to regional classification. The proposed Stacking framework achieves an overall localization accuracy of 0.9667 and a Macro-F1 score of 0.9666, with relatively stable diagnostic performance under pressure noise. Global SHAP analysis identifies several monitoring nodes with substantial contributions to the overall classification, while the observed misclassifications are mainly concentrated between a small number of neighboring regions with similar feature representations. A further test on the Net3 network achieves an overall localization accuracy of 0.9812, providing additional evidence of the applicability of the framework across different network configurations. Overall, the results indicate that the proposed framework provides an accurate and interpretable approach for leakage localization in WDNs.\n\nKeywords:\n\nWDNs; SHAP; leakage localization; ensemble learning; model interpretability\n\n## 1. Introduction\n\nA water distribution network (WDN) is an engineering system composed of water sources, pipelines, pumping stations, and storage facilities. Its primary function is to deliver treated water resources to various users according to demand. Such systems play a critical role in ensuring the continuity of water supply and water quality safety, and are of great importance to public health, urban operation, and emergency response. During long-term operation, WDNs are subjected to various adverse factors, such as pipeline aging, corrosion, external load variations, and construction disturbances. These factors gradually weaken the structural strength and sealing performance of pipelines, thereby increasing the probability of leakage occurrence within the network. According to the development characteristics of leakage, water network leakage can generally be classified into two categories: burst leakage and progressive (hidden) leakage [1]. Burst leakage refers to leakage events caused by sudden structural failure of pipelines within a very short period of time, such as pipe bursts, joint fractures, or external damage. This type of leakage is typically characterized by its abrupt occurrence, large leakage flow rate, and significant disturbance to the hydraulic state of the system, often accompanied by rapid pressure drops and abnormal flow fluctuations within the network. In contrast, progressive (hidden) leakage develops gradually as a result of pipeline aging, corrosion, or deterioration of sealing performance. At the early stage, the leakage rate is relatively low and has limited influence on hydraulic parameters, making it difficult to trigger obvious pressure or flow anomalies; however, the leakage severity tends to increase progressively over time. Once leakage occurs in a WDN, it not only leads to water resource waste and reduced water supply efficiency, but may also cause abnormal network pressure and secondary water contamination, thereby affecting water supply safety and the operational stability of the system [2]. Therefore, in order to reduce water losses and operational risks caused by leakage and to enhance the safety and stability of WDNs, it is of great significance to conduct effective research on leakage detection and localization.\n\nPrevious studies on leakage detection have mainly focused on three categories of methods: model-based methods, statistical methods, and machine learning methods [3,4,5]. Model-based methods establish hydraulic models of WDNs to simulate the expected sensor readings under normal operating conditions, and then compare these predicted values with the actual monitoring data collected by Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (SCADA) systems. When significant discrepancies (residuals) occur between the predicted and measured values, these residuals can be used to determine the existence of leakage within the system and further infer the leakage location based on their spatial distribution characteristics. For example, Keramat, et al. [6] proposed a matched-filter objective function combined with the inverse transient method to detect leakage in WDNs under measurement noise conditions. Levinas, et al. [7] developed a novel method based on transient modeling of water networks, capable of generating high-resolution pressure responses under various scenarios to identify leaking pipes in pressurized WDNs. Vrachimis, et al. [8] proposed a leakage detection and localization approach using interval models, which achieved higher detection rates compared with similar methods employing bounded uncertainties. Although these model-based approaches demonstrate strong theoretical rigor, hydraulic models are often difficult to accurately establish and continuously update in practical applications. In addition, uncertainties in parameters such as pipe roughness, pipe diameter, and nodal water demand, together with the high computational cost, limit their applicability in large-scale and real-time scenarios. Furthermore, some high-performing model-driven approaches rely heavily on an in-depth understanding of the hydraulic characteristics of WDNs and high-quality model calibration, conditions that are often difficult to satisfy in real-world applications. Hydraulic transient analysis also requires extremely high temporal resolution of monitoring data, typically at the second or even millisecond level, which existing SCADA systems generally cannot provide. This mismatch further constrains the practical implementation of such methods [4,9,10,11].\n\nStatistical methods reduce the dependence on hydraulic models and alleviate the burden associated with model calibration by directly analyzing variations and anomalies in observed operational data, such as flow and pressure measurements collected from SCADA systems. Among these approaches, statistical process control (SPC) methods are widely applied for leakage detection due to their simplicity, computational efficiency, and suitability for real-time monitoring. Representative SPC techniques include the Western Electric Company rules [12], cumulative sum (CUSUM) method [13], exponentially weighted moving average (EWMA) method [14,15], and Hotelling’s T^{2} method [16]. Specifically, rule-based approaches identify abnormal operating conditions by detecting deviations from predefined thresholds, while CUSUM and EWMA methods improve sensitivity to small or persistent changes by accumulating and smoothing measurement deviations. Hotelling’s T^{2} method extends statistical monitoring to multivariate scenarios by considering correlations among multiple measurements. In addition, Bayesian theory-based statistical approaches have also been investigated for leakage detection, where prior knowledge and probabilistic inference are incorporated to quantify uncertainties and improve decision-making under incomplete information [17]. Despite these advantages, statistical methods still face several limitations. Most conventional statistical approaches rely on assumptions that SCADA data follow known or approximately Gaussian distributions, which may not hold under complex operating conditions [4]. Moreover, their detection performance can be affected by varying demand patterns, sensor noise, and other non-leakage-related disturbances. These limitations restrict their ability to capture highly nonlinear relationships and complex interactions among multiple influencing factors.\n\nMachine learning methods have demonstrated strong capabilities in automatically learning complex patterns from large-scale and high-dimensional datasets, capturing nonlinear relationships among multiple variables, and adapting to diverse data characteristics. Among various monitoring signals, pressure and flow measurements are the most commonly used data sources in water distribution systems. Compared with flow meters, pressure sensors are generally easier to install, require lower investment, and can be deployed more extensively throughout the network. Therefore, pressure-based methods have attracted increasing attention for leakage identification and localization. The underlying principle is that pipe leakage disturbs the hydraulic equilibrium of the WDN, typically resulting in pressure reductions and deviations from normal operating patterns. By analyzing the discrepancies between real-time pressure observations and expected hydraulic conditions, leakage events can be detected, and their potential locations can be inferred [26]. For example, Farley et al. [27] utilized hydraulic model simulations to optimize pressure sensor locations by quantifying the sensitivity of different monitoring points to potential burst events, enabling effective burst localization with a limited number of pressure instruments. Duan et al. [28] reviewed transient-based methods for water distribution system diagnosis, where transient pressure responses generated by hydraulic disturbances were analyzed to identify leakage and other pipeline defects.\n\nIn recent years, ensemble learning has gained increasing attention in machine learning applications due to its ability to improve predictive performance and robustness by combining multiple base learners. In the field of water distribution systems, ensemble learning approaches have been increasingly explored for leakage detection and localization by integrating heterogeneous models and capturing complex relationships between hydraulic responses and leakage characteristics. For example, Zhou, et al. [29] developed an ensemble transfer learning one-dimensional convolutional neural network (TL1DCNN) for pipeline leak detection and localization by integrating multiple CNN-based base learners, achieving improved localization performance compared with individual models. Farghadan, et al. [30] proposed an ensemble Bayesian classifier framework for leak localization in water distribution networks by integrating multiple classifiers to identify leak locations under sensor limitations and model uncertainties. Ravichandran, et al. [31] proposed a gradient boosting tree-based ensemble learning approach for acoustic leak detection, which improved detection accuracy and robustness compared with conventional machine learning models. Fuentes and Pedrasa [32] developed an ensemble machine learning framework for WDN leak detection, demonstrating the potential of combining multiple ML models to improve leakage identification performance.\n\nAlthough significant progress has been achieved in leakage detection and localization research, several challenges remain to be addressed. First, existing studies have demonstrated promising performance in leakage localization; however, comparatively limited attention has been devoted to robust multi-region localization classification in WDNs with complex topologies, particularly under operational uncertainties. Second, although various machine learning techniques have been increasingly applied to leakage detection and localization, the potential of ensemble learning to integrate the complementary strengths of different algorithms and improve localization robustness has received comparatively limited attention. In addition, the interpretability of data-driven leakage localization models remains an important consideration, as water utilities require not only accurate localization results but also an understanding of the monitoring information and hydraulic patterns underlying model decisions. Finally, existing studies have explored spatial, topological, and hydraulic information for leakage analysis from different perspectives, but a systematic framework that explicitly couples spatial/topological properties with hydraulic response characteristics for network zoning and subsequent localization classification remains relatively underexplored. These observations motivate the development of an interpretable ensemble learning framework that integrates spatial–hydraulic information for robust multi-region leakage localization in WDNs. This study proposes the following objectives:\n\n  * To construct a WDN partitioning model that deeply couples spatial and hydraulic features, utilizing spatial distance and hydraulic similarity indicators to achieve rational network partitioning and provide physical support for accurate leakage localization.\n\n  * To develop and compare multiple localization frameworks based on different machine learning models and ensemble strategies, systematically evaluating the differences in localization accuracy and robustness among Random Forest (RF), XGBoost, Multilayer Perceptron (MLP), ensemble learning approaches including Voting and Stacking constructed from these base models, and an independently trained LightGBM model.\n\n  * To introduce the SHAP (SHapley Additive exPlanations) analysis method for multidimensional model interpretation and combine it with confusion matrix analysis to reveal the intrinsic relationships between pressure residuals, leakage regions, and model decision-making behavior from both global and local perspectives.\n\n  * To evaluate the robustness and noise tolerance of the proposed framework, and to assess the stability of SHAP feature importance under varying levels of measurement uncertainty, thereby confirming its reliability and interpretability for practical leakage localization.\n\nBy achieving these objectives, this study aims to bridge the gap between the predictive capability of machine learning and the interpretability requirements of the water utility industry. Through accurate localization prediction and transparent decision analysis, the proposed framework is expected to provide technical support for the efficient operation and maintenance of WDNs.\n\n## 2. Research Methodology\n\nTo construct a high-accuracy, robust, and interpretable leakage localization framework for urban water distribution networks (WDNs), this study proposes an integrated methodology combining spatial–hydraulic regional partitioning, stochastic scenario generation, heterogeneous model ensembling, and multi-dimensional interpretability analysis. As illustrated in Figure 1, the overall workflow systematically progresses through four interconnected stages: first, coupling spatial topological distances and hydraulic head similarities to divide the network into physically meaningful operational zones; second, simulating diverse leakage scenarios via Monte Carlo sampling on virtual mid-pipe nodes during nighttime low-demand conditions and applying Pearson correlation alongside ANOVA feature selection to identify an optimal, non-redundant sensor subset; third, benchmarking heterogeneous base learners (RF, XGBoost, and MLP) against an independent LightGBM baseline, and fusing their complementary mechanisms using Voting and Stacking ensemble architectures (with a Logistic Regression meta-learner); and finally, evaluating localization accuracy and robustness under measurement noise perturbations while employing KernelExplainer SHAP analysis and confusion matrix cross-examination to uncover the underlying hydraulic logic governing model predictions.\nIn this study, burst leakage and progressive leakage were not explicitly modeled as separate leakage mechanisms. Instead, all leakage events were generated using the same scenario generation framework, with leakage severity represented by variations in the emitter coefficient. This simplification was adopted to evaluate the effectiveness of the proposed monitoring point optimization method while avoiding additional assumptions regarding leakage evolution.\n\nTo highlight the local disturbances caused by leakage, this paper uses the pressure residual as a characteristic quantity, which helps enhance the model’s sensitivity to leakage-induced hydraulic disturbances. It is defined as:\n\n$$P{R}_{i}={P}_{i}^{N}-{P}_{i}^{L}$$\n\n(2)\n\nwhere $P{R}_{i}$ denotes the pressure residual at node i, and ${P}_{i}^{N}$ is the pressure under normal operating conditions, and ${P}_{i}^{L}$ is the pressure under leakage conditions.\n\n### 2.2. Water Distribution Network Division\n\nTo achieve a reasonable partitioning of the pipeline network structure and support subsequent leakage localization analysis, this study proposes a regional partitioning method integrating spatial information and hydraulic characteristics. ### 2.3. Prediction Model\n\nThis study selected RF, XGBoost, and MLP as representative base learners considering their complementary modeling characteristics and widespread adoption in infrastructure-related prediction tasks. RF was included as a bagging-based tree ensemble that provides robustness against noise and feature variability, while XGBoost was selected as a boosting-based model with strong capability in capturing complex nonlinear relationships and feature interactions. MLP was introduced as a neural network-based learner to represent nonlinear patterns through hierarchical feature transformations. These models were selected to provide diverse learning mechanisms, including tree-based bagging, gradient boosting, and neural representation learning, thereby enabling a comprehensive evaluation of different modeling paradigms. In addition, LightGBM was included as an independent benchmark model owing to its efficient gradient boosting framework and strong predictive performance, providing an additional reference for performance comparison with the proposed ensemble approaches. \n#### 2.3.2. Gradient Boosting Decision Tree (GBDT)\n\nGradient boosting decision tree (GBDT) models build an ensemble of decision trees sequentially, with each newly added tree trained to correct the residual errors of the existing ensemble. In this study, XGBoost a…605 tokens truncated…g procedures involving statistical estimation. Feature selection based on Pearson correlation analysis and ANOVA F-values was performed solely on the training set, and the resulting feature subset was then applied to the testing set. Likewise, feature standardization parameters (mean and standard deviation) were estimated from the training set and subsequently used to transform both the training and testing sets. Therefore, no information from the testing set was involved during model development, ensuring an unbiased evaluation of model performance.\n\nBased on this analysis, redundant features were systematically removed. For highly correlated feature pairs, the feature with the higher F-value and greater classification contribution was preferentially retained. Furthermore, the geographic and spatial distribution of the nodes across the 7 partitions was carefully cross-verified during the elimination process to avoid spatial clustering of sensors and ensure comprehensive monitoring coverage over the entire network topology. Consequently, the feature dimension was successfully reduced from the initial 33 candidate points to an optimal subset of 8 monitoring points. This process successfully eliminates feature redundancy while preserving critical information as much as possible, thereby improving both the generalization ability and computational efficiency of the model. Figure 7 presents the correlation matrix heatmap of these 8 final retained monitoring points, which serve as the definitive input features for the leak localization models.\n\n### 3.2. Model Evaluation\n\nHyperparameter optimization was performed for all individual models. Grid search was not adopted because its exhaustive evaluation of all parameter combinations would result in high computational costs. Therefore, Random Search and Optuna were compared in preliminary experiments. As shown in Figure 8 and Table 1, both methods achieved comparable macro-F1 scores, while Random Search showed a time advantage. Thus, Random Search was selected for the final hyperparameter optimization. For each model, 100 randomly sampled parameter combinations were evaluated using five-fold cross-validation, with the mean macro-F1 score as the optimization criterion. The optimized hyperparameters are summarized in Table 2.\n\nImage\n\nFigure 8. Performance comparison of Random Search and Optuna: (a) Random Forest, (b) XGBoost, (c) MLP, and (d) LightGBM.\n\nImage\n\nTable 1. Hyperparameter optimization time.\n\nImage\n\nTable 2. Hyperparameter configurations.\n\nFor the Stacking ensemble model, RF, XGBoost, and MLP were selected as heterogeneous base-learners due to their complementary learning behaviors, while Logistic Regression was employed as the second-stage meta-learner. An out-of-fold (OOF) prediction strategy based on 5-fold cross-validation was strictly adopted to generate meta-features for training the meta-learner, thereby preventing data snooping and information leakage. In addition, a heterogeneous Voting ensemble model was developed using the same three optimized base learners through a soft-voting mechanism that averages the predicted class probabilities. To further provide a comprehensive benchmark, an optimized LightGBM model was independently implemented as an additional gradient boosting-based comparison model. The performance of all optimized individual models (RF, XGBoost, MLP, and LightGBM) and ensemble architectures (Stacking and Voting) was finally evaluated on an independent test set. The detailed performance metrics are summarized in Table 3, and the quantitative comparisons among different models are illustrated in Figure 9.\n## 4. Discussion\n\nL-Town is a well-established and widely used benchmark network for leakage detection and localization in water distribution systems. Originally developed for the BattLeDIM competition, it has since been extensively adopted as a standardized testbed for evaluating and comparing different leakage detection and localization methods. For example, during the competition, the Cheng00 team employed change-point detection and statistical analysis for leakage detection and localization [50], the D and W team used pressure sensitivity analysis to identify potentially leaking pipes [51], and the Leakbusters team combined data-driven pressure reconstruction with hydraulic simulation [52]. Following the competition, a broader range of approaches have been investigated. Rajabi, et al. [53] proposed a method based on a Conditional Deep Convolutional Generative Adversarial Network (CDCGAN) and the Structural Similarity Index Measure (SSIM) for real-time leak detection and localization on L-Town, achieving approximately 70% detection accuracy, while Wang, et al. [54] developed a hydraulic-model-free approach based on graph theory and Bayesian reasoning, with localization errors below 300 m for all 10 leak points and below 70 m in some cases. Meanwhile, network partitioning has emerged as an important strategy for simplifying leakage localization. Dong, et al. [55] combined three-order embedding, K-means clustering, and Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) for network partitioning and leak localization, incorporating pressure sensitivity, spatial information, and network connectivity, while Pérez-Sandoval, et al. [56] integrated Spectral Clustering with Bidirectional Long Short-Term Memory (Bi-LSTM) and achieved 85% effectiveness across 117 unseen leakage scenarios on L-Town.\n\nWithin this research landscape, the present study focuses on partitioning-based leakage localization while further addressing the prediction stage through an interpretable Stacking ensemble framework. Rather than depending on a single learning architecture, the proposed approach integrates RF, MLP, and XGBoost to exploit complementary predictive characteristics, and further reduces the sensing requirement through an optimized subset of eight pressure sensors. On the L-Town benchmark, the framework achieves an overall localization accuracy of 0.9667 and a Macro-F1 score of 0.9666, demonstrating strong localization performance. \nNevertheless, several limitations should be acknowledged. First, the present evaluation was conducted primarily on the L-Town benchmark network, with the Net3 network used for additional validation. Although these two networks provide different network configurations and support the assessment of the generalizability of the proposed framework, they cannot fully represent the structural, hydraulic, operational, and sensor-related characteristics of real-world water distribution networks. Second, the leakage scenarios were generated based on predefined parameter ranges and simulation settings; therefore, uncertainties associated with actual leakage characteristics, demand variations, measurement errors, hydraulic parameter uncertainty, and operational changes may not be fully captured. Third, the proposed framework relies on pressure measurements from a limited number of monitoring points and on the partitioning strategy used to define the localization regions. Consequently, its performance may depend on sensor placement and network-specific hydraulic characteristics. Finally, although the results demonstrate strong performance on the evaluated benchmark networks, further validation using additional networks, field measurements, and experimentally or operationally observed leakage events is necessary to determine the robustness of the framework under diverse real-world conditions. Future research will therefore focus on extending the evaluation to a broader range of WDN topologies and operational conditions, incorporating greater uncertainty in hydraulic and leakage parameters, and validating the proposed framework using real-world monitoring data.\n\n## 5. Conclusions\n\nThis study developed a spatial–hydraulic coupled and interpretable ensemble learning framework for leakage localization in urban WDNs. By combining spatial topology and hydraulic similarity, the framework delineated meaningful zones for leakage isolation, while the Stacking ensemble showed improved diagnostic performance over individual models and conventional Voting methods. SHAP analysis identified several monitoring nodes with substantial contributions to the regional classification, while the observed misclassifications were mainly concentrated between a small number of neighboring regions with similar feature representations. These results provide an interpretable view of the contributions of individual monitoring nodes to the classification process. An extended case study on the Net3 network further showed that the same framework achieved satisfactory localization performance under a different network configuration, providing additional evidence of its applicability across different WDN structures. Sensitivity analysis further indicated relatively stable diagnostic performance and feature-importance rankings under sensor noise of up to 20%. Overall, the framework shows potential for interpretable and robust leakage localization in practical WDN applications.\n\nDespite these promising results, several methodological limitations remain to be addressed. First, the framework was validated using the synthetic L-Town benchmark network under nighttime steady-state conditions, assuming single-partition leakage scenarios without considering real-time demand dynamics or multiple simultaneous leaks. Second, model development relied on simulated EPANET data, which may not fully reflect sensor drift, missing data, or dynamic topology changes encountered in real operational environments.\n\nTo overcome these limitations, future research will focus on expanding the framework to accommodate unsteady-state daytime hydraulics, non-stationary demand fluctuations, and complex scenarios involving concurrent leakage events across multiple partitions. Additionally, incorporating domain-specific physical constraints directly into the ensemble architecture and testing against real-world operational datasets will be crucial. Future efforts will also explore adaptive sensor placement algorithms and transfer learning methodologies to facilitate seamless deployment across heterogeneous WDN topologies without retraining from scratch. From an operational perspective, the proposed framework offers significant practical relevance for water utility management. By translating black-box machine learning outputs into transparent, SHAP-guided spatial diagnostics, utility operators can rapidly isolate leakage-prone zones, optimize pipe repair schedules, and minimize non-revenue water (NRW) losses. Furthermore, the non-redundant sensor feature selection methodology provides a cost-effective strategy for utilities seeking to upgrade sparse monitoring infrastructures under strict capital expenditure constraints.\n\n## Author Contributions\n\nConceptualization, Q.L. and A.C.; methodology, A.C.; software, A.C.; validation, Q.L. and A.C.; formal analysis, A.C.; investigation, Q.L.; resources, Q.L.; data curation, Q.L.; writing—original draft preparation, A.C.; writing—review and editing, Q.L.; visualization, A.C.; supervision, Q.L.; project administration, Q.L.; funding acquisition, Q.L. Given the efficiency of the data-driven approach and the intricate node-line structure of water networks, graph-based machine learning methods offer a promising approach. However, the scarcity of real-time flow acquisition devices limits the ability to directly forecast timely demand. We evaluated the three models on four benchmark WDNs: Anytown, C-Town, Richmond, and L-Town, described in Section cite158†5.1 . The experiments were conducted following the method proposed by Hajgató et al. (cite104†2020 ) to ensure a fair comparison.\nL543: Specifically, 1,000, 10,000, and 20,000 snapshots were generated for the C-Town, L-Town, and Richmond WDNs, respectively. Then, the data sets were split into training, validation, and test sets in a 6:2:2 ratio.\nL544: We used the same experimental settings as proposed in the baseline approaches to guarantee a fair comparison. Thus, in all experiments the models are trained for 2,000 epochs with early stopping, using the Adam gradient-based optimization algorithm (Kingma & Ba, cite127†2014 ). The GraphConvWat model training was stopped if the validation loss did not improve for 50 consecutive epochs. In the case of mGCN, the training was stopped if no improvement is seen after 250 epochs.\nL545: Likewise mGCN, our model training is stopped after 250 epochs if no improvement is observed. In previous approaches, the snapshots representing random WDN states used for training, validation and test are created by the same algorithm. Consequently, the distribution of the data used for testing is a fidelity copy of the data used for training.\nL564: However, in practice, the distribution of the real data differs from the data used for training the reconstruction models, as explained in Section cite143†2.2 . Therefore, it is important that the models adapt to circumvent such uncertainties. Previous approaches achieve impressive performance when tested on replicas of the training data (see Table cite160†11 ), but the performance drop is evident when they are evaluated on a realistic scenario (see Table cite159†10 ).\nL565: Table 11. 44     def _partial_fit(self, x, y):
45         self.STMX.append(x)
46         self.STMy.append(y)
47         self.sampleCount += 1
48 
49         # build up initial LTM
50         if len(self.LTMX) < self.n_neighbors:
51             self.LTMX.append(x)
52             self.LTMy.append(y)
53 
54         if len(self.STMX) < self.n_neighbors:
55             return
56 
57         self._evaluateMemories(x, y)
58         self._adaptSTM()
59         self._cleanLTM(x, y)
60         self._enforceMaxLTMSize()

62     def _enforceMaxLTMSize(self):
63 
64         while (len(self.LTMX) > self.max_LTM_size):
65             ltm_tree = KDTree(np.array(self.LTMX))
66             dist, ind = ltm_tree.query(np.array(self.LTMX), k=2)
67             dist = dist[:, 1]
68             ind = ind[:, 1]
69 
70             min_idx = np.argmin(dist)
71             idx = ind[min_idx]
72 
73             self.LTMX = np.delete(self.LTMX, idx, axis=0).tolist()
74             self.LTMy = np.delete(self.LTMy, idx, axis=0).tolist()

454 class GResBlockMeanConv(torch.nn.Module):
455     def __init__(self, in_dim, out_dim, hc):
456         super(GResBlockMeanConv, self).__init__()
457 
458         self.conv1 = GATConv(in_dim, hc, 2, concat=True)
459         self.conv2 = GATConv(hc * 2, out_dim, 1, concat=False)
460         self.mean_conv = SimpleConv(aggr="mean")
461 
462     def forward(self, x, edge_index, edge_attr=None):
463         x_0 = torch.clone(x)
464         x = self.conv1(x, edge_index, edge_attr).relu()
465         x = self.conv2(x, edge_index, edge_attr)
466         x = self.mean_conv(x, edge_index) + x_0
467         x = F.relu(x)
468         return x
469 
470 
471 class GATResMeanConv(torch.nn.Module):
472     def __init__(self, name='GATResMeanConv', num_blocks=5, nc = 32):
473         super(GATResMeanConv, self).__init__()
474 
475         self.num_blocks = num_blocks
476 
477         self.lin0 = Linear(1, nc)
478         self.blocks = torch.nn.ModuleList()
479         self.name = name
480         for _ in range(self.num_blocks):
481             block = GResBlockMeanConv(nc, nc, nc)
482             self.blocks.append(block)
483 
484         self.lin1 = Linear(nc, 1)
485 
486     def forward(self, x, edge_index, batch=None, edge_attr=None):
487         x = self.lin0(x)
488 
489         for i in range(self.num_blocks):
490             x = self.blocks[i](x, edge_index, edge_attr)
491 
492         x = self.lin1(x)
493         #x = torch.sigmoid(x)
494         return x

SAM PAGE 7 SAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning in Water Distribution Networks
7
Algorithm 1 SAM-kNN Regression
Input: Data stream S, one si at a time
Output: ˆyi for every si
1: MST , MLT = {s0, .., sLmin}
▷Initialize STM and LTM
2: EST , ELT , EC = 0
▷Initialize tracked errors
3: for si ∈S \ {s0, .., sLmin} do
▷Loop over the remaining data stream
4:
BM = argmin(EST , ELT , EC)
▷Find best memory with lowest error
5:
ˆyi = kNNBM(si)
▷Predict with kNN of best memory
6:
Update EST , ELT , EC
7:
MST = MST ∪{si}
▷Add current sample to STM
8:
Evaluate smaller STM sizes
9:
if STM is reduced then
10:
Ot = MSTt \ MSTt+1
▷Take discarded samples from STM
11:
clean(Ot, MSTt+1)
▷Clean discarded samples with respect to new STM
12:
MLT = MLT ∪clean(Ot, MSTt+1)
▷Add cleaned samples to LTM
13:
clean(MLT , MSTt+1)
▷Clean new LTM with respect to new STM
14:
end if
15: end for
5
Experiments
We empirically evaluate our proposed method in an online scenario for detecting
leakages and sensor faults in water distribution networks – all experiments are
implemented in Python§.
5.1
Data
We use a version of the L-Town water distribution network as used in [16], as a
prominent realistic benchmark for anomaly detection – we use Area A only which
consists of 661 nodes, 766 links, and 29 (optimally placed) pressure sensors. We
build and simulate 10 scenarios where the ﬁrst 5 scenarios each contain a single
leakage – we vary position, time and size of the leakage – and the remaining 5
scenarios each contain a single diﬀerent sensor fault (position is varied):
– Scenario 6: Sensor measurement is overﬂowing over time – i.e. it is going to
inﬁnity over time.
– Scenario 7: Gaussian noise is added to the sensor measurement.
– Scenario 8: A constant value is added to the sensor measurement.
– Scenario 9: Sensor measurement is set equal to zero.
– Scenario 10: Sensor measurement is shifted by a small amount.
Each scenario is simulated (using WNTR [8]) for 3 month and pressure sensors
are sampled every 5 minutes.
§Implementation is available on GitHub: https://github.com/andreArtelt/
SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs

```text
229     def _evaluateMemories(self, x, y):
230         # absolute Mean Error Calculation
231         if (self.error_type == 'MAE'):
232             if(self.multi_dim_y == False):
233                 self.modelError = ((self.sampleCount - 1) * self.modelError + abs(self.predict([x]) - y)) / self.sampleCount
234                 STMsize = len(self.STMX)
235                 self.STMerror = ((STMsize - 1) * self.STMerror + abs(self.STMpredict(x) - y)) / STMsize
236                 LTMsize = len(self.LTMX)
237                 self.LTMerror = ((LTMsize - 1) * self.LTMerror + abs(self.LTMpredict(x) - y)) / LTMsize
238                 size = STMsize + LTMsize
239                 self.COMBerror = ((size - 1) * self.COMBerror + abs(self.COMBpredict(x) - y)) / size
240             else:
241                 self.modelError = ((self.sampleCount - 1) * self.modelError + abs(np.sum(self.predict([x]) - y)) / y.shape[0]) / self.sampleCount
242                 STMsize = len(self.STMX)
243                 self.STMerror = ((STMsize - 1) * self.STMerror + abs(np.sum(self.STMpredict(x) - y)) / y.shape[0]) / STMsize
244                 LTMsize = len(self.LTMX)
245                 self.LTMerror = ((LTMsize - 1) * self.LTMerror + abs(np.sum(self.LTMpredict(x) - y)) / y.shape[0]) / LTMsize
246                 size = STMsize + LTMsize
247                 self.COMBerror = ((size - 1) * self.COMBerror + abs(np.sum(self.COMBpredict(x) - y)) / y.shape[0]) / size
248 
249         # Root Mean Squared Error Calculation
250         elif (self.error_type == 'RMSE'):
251             if (self.multi_dim_y == False):
252                 self.modelError = np.sqrt(((self.sampleCount - 1) * np.square(self.modelError) + np.square(self.predict([x]) - y)) / self.sampleCount)
253                 STMsize = len(self.STMX)
254                 self.STMerror = np.sqrt(((STMsize - 1) * np.square(self.STMerror) + np.square(self.STMpredict(x) - y)) / STMsize)
255                 LTMsize = len(self.LTMX)
256                 self.LTMerror = np.sqrt(((LTMsize - 1) * np.square(self.LTMerror) + np.square(self.LTMpredict(x) - y)) / LTMsize)
257                 size = STMsize + LTMsize
258                 self.COMBerror = np.sqrt(((size - 1) * np.square(self.COMBerror) + np.square(self.COMBpredict(x) - y)) / size)
259             else:
260                 self.modelError = np.sqrt(((self.sampleCount - 1) * np.square(self.modelError) + np.square(np.sum(self.predict([x]) - y)) / y.shape[0]) / self.sampleCount)
261                 STMsize = len(self.STMX)
262                 self.STMerror = np.sqrt(((STMsize - 1) * np.square(self.STMerror) + np.square(np.sum(self.STMpredict(x) - y)) / y.shape[0]) / STMsize)
263                 LTMsize = len(self.LTMX)
264                 self.LTMerror = np.sqrt(((LTMsize - 1) * np.square(self.LTMerror) + np.square(np.sum(self.LTMpredict(x) - y)) / y.shape[0]) / LTMsize)
265                 size = STMsize + LTMsize
266                 self.COMBerror = np.sqrt(((size - 1) * np.square(self.COMBerror) + np.square(np.sum(self.COMBpredict(x) - y)) / y.shape[0]) / size)
267 
268     def _adaptSTM(self):
269         STMX = np.array(self.STMX)
270         STMy = np.array(self.STMy)
331     def predict(self, X):
332         """ predict
333 
334         Predicts the value of the X sample, by searching the KDTree for
335         the n_neighbors-Nearest Neighbors.
336 
337         Parameters
338         ----------
339         X: Numpy.ndarray of shape (n_samples, n_features)
340             All the samples we want to predict the label for.
341 
342         Returns
343         -------
344         list
345             A list containing the predicted values for all instances in X.
346 
347         """
348 
349         mem_list = [self.STMerror, self.LTMerror, self.COMBerror]
350         best_mem_ind = np.argmin(mem_list)
351         if (best_mem_ind != self.best_mem):
352             self.best_mem = best_mem_ind
353         if (best_mem_ind == 0):
354             return np.array([self.STMpredict(x) for x in X])
355         elif (best_mem_ind == 1):
356             return np.array([self.LTMpredict(x) for x in X])
357         elif (best_mem_ind == 2):
358             return np.array([self.COMBpredict(x) for x in X])
359 
360     def fit(self, X, y):
361         if (len(self.STMX) < self.n_neighbors):
362             self.LTMX = list(X[0:10, :])
363             self.STMX = list(X[0:10, :])
364             self.LTMy = list(y[0:10])
365             self.STMy = list(y[0:10])
366             self.sampleCount = 10
367             self.partial_fit(X[10:, :], y[10:])
368         else:
369             self.partial_fit(X, y)
370 
371     def predict_proba(self):
372         pass
371     def predict_proba(self):
372         pass
373 
374     def print_model(self):
375         print("Mean Absolute Errors:  Complete Model:", self.modelError, "STM: ", self.STMerror / len(self.STMX),
376               "  LTM: ", self.LTMerror, "  COMB: ", self.COMBerror)
```

### aggregated_output

```text

REF 8
PAGE 4 During model calibration, regression coefficients k0 ji, k1 ji, and kd ji are estimated using a least-squares method (Montgomery 2020) on a manually selected time period where no leaks have occurred. LI-2: Reconstruction Error Analysis When comparing the time series of the node pressure estimated with the linear regression model ˆPi formulated in Eq. (12) with the observed pressure values Pi by analyzing their difference [com- pare Eq. (13)], additional occurring flows, i.e., leakages, contribute to an increase of the model error. They can be regarded in Eq. (12) as part of additional demand in the WDN. This yields the following definition of the MRE: MREji ¼ Pi −ˆPi ¼ Pi −  k0 ji þ Pjk1 ji þ X d∈DIR kd;jiQ2 d  ¼ X l∈L kl;jiQ2 l ð13Þ where L denotes all leakages. Under the assumptions that no leak- ages are present in the network and that measurement noise is white noise, MRE is expected to be normally distributed with a mean value of 0 for all time steps tno leak. Its expectation value Eð·Þ will be E½MREjiðtno leakÞ ¼ 0. The pressure drop ΔP ¼ PðtleakÞ −Pðtno leakÞ caused at a certain location in the WDN by a leakage can be calculated as the difference of the pressure value observed at that location in the two time steps before (tno leak) and after (tleak) the leak start. Considering a leakage ls occurring at a location with corresponding MAS s, the pressure drop at s due to the leakage will be greater in magnitude than for all other sensor locations. Because the pressure drop is negative in sign, the following relation is obtained: ΔPs < ΔPi; ∀i ≠s ð14Þ When combining Eqs. (13) and (14) and choosing s ¼ j, the effect of the leakage ls will cause exclusively positive trends in the MREsi, such that E½MREsiðtleakÞ > 0, ∀i ≠s, thereby ena- bling identification of the MAS sðtÞ ¼ arg maxj X i signðMREjiðtÞÞ  ð15Þ Furthermore, let the vector MREs be represented by the MRE for the combination of node s with all nodes i; then the magnitude of that vector may be calculated with the Frobenius-norm k · kF (cf. Golub and Van Loan 2013). Finally, the detection of a single new leak occurring in the WDN, along with its starting time, may be accomplished by applying change point detection (Step LI-3) to the time series kMREsðtÞkF. After a leak is definitively identified and becomes distinct, the model may be recalibrated to be reset for new leakage identification. Multiple simultaneous leaks will also be reflected in the time series kMREsðtÞkF in an aggregated form [compare Eq. (13)]. However, disaggregation into individual leaks is not straightfor- ward in the current implementation of LILA. Thus, for a more pre- cise analysis of each individual leak within the overall aggregated error, we manually selected, for each leak, an individual combina- tion of nodes that revealed the leak signal in the most distinct fashion. LI-3: Change Point Detection The last task of leakage identification is the detection of variations in the MRE time series induced by a leak flow; therefore, this rep- resents a change point detection problem. Change point detection problems have been widely studied in the literature, and there is a multitude of statistical methods available. For a comprehensive review we refer the reader to Aminikhanghahi and Cook (2017). As explained in the previous section, we expect the MRE to be unbiased and normally distributed for the network state without leaks. With the start of a leak event, the error mean will either abruptly or gradually shift away from zero, depending on the nature of the leak. Successful change point detection aims to flag these instances as early as possible, while at the same time avoiding false alarms. In LILA, we apply the widely used Cusum algorithm (Montgomery 2020), known from statistical process monitoring, to automatically detect changes in the MRE. The Cusum algorithm is a method to detect changes in the prob- ability distribution of an univariate signal. It accumulates the dis- tance Ci of the target variable x from its mean μx, corrected by a slack value K, which is chosen based on the change of mean that is to be detected. Often K is selected based on the estimated standard deviation σx of the target variable multiplied by a parameter δ [cf. Eq. (16)]. A change point is flagged once Ci exceeds a prede- fined threshold H ¼ η × σx. In general, the algorithm comes in two forms for detecting positive and negative changes from the mean. For our application, we use the formulation to detect changes in the positive direction because leaks will manifest themselves as posi- tive values in the MRE (cf. section LI-2: Reconstruction Error Analysis) Cþ i ¼ max½0; xi −ðμ0 þ KÞ þ Cþ i−1 with Cþ 0 ¼ 0 and K ¼ δ 2 · σx ð16Þ To evaluate the general suitability of the Cusum method for the leak identification problem, we created artificial error trajectories by adding different levels of white noise to given ground truth leak trajectories. This allowed us to confirm the algorithm’s ability t

(12)) features, respectively:(11)̃𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖=̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝐹⁡ℎ⁡(𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)(11-a)𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖=𝑄𝐹⁡ℎ⁡(𝑐(𝑙)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)·𝐾𝐹⁡ℎ⁡(𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)√𝐷(11-b)̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=exp⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)(12)̃𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖=̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝐹⁡𝑡⁡(𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)(12-a)𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖=𝑄𝐹⁡𝑡⁡(𝑐(𝑙)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)·𝐾𝐹⁡𝑡⁡(𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)√𝐷(12-b)̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=exp⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)

Manag., 142 (2016), Article 04016044, 10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0000691\n### 2.3. Cascade masking operation\n\nPrior research [18] demonstrates that masking operations improve model generalization in water network applications. The core strategy involves randomly selecting a proportion of sensor nodes’ input during training. These inputs are then set to zero, simulating prediction scenarios for non-monitored sites. This approach effectively enhances the model’s ability to generalize to indirectly observed nodes, making it an effective method for data augmentation.\n\nBuilding on this concept, this study introduces the cascade masking operation as a novel enhancement strategy. Renowned for its multi-level refinement in complex image processing tasks [2,3], such as instance segmentation, cascade masking can progressively enhance both segmentation accuracy and robustness. This study adapts this advanced concept to graph processing by incrementally decreasing the number of randomly masked sensor measurements and their features during training. This staged approach systematically strengthens the model's ability to capture complex interdependencies between sensor data and network topology, significantly improving generalization to unmonitored nodes.\n\n### 4.2. Results explanation\n\n#### 4.2.1. Rule extraction from recurrent layer\n\nEmploying recurrent graph convolution (as shown in Eq. (5)), the AGN model gradually expands the receptive field of sensor nodes’ neighboring nodes. Simultaneously, pressure feature transfer from sensor nodes to global nodes is achieved by the self-attention mechanism, enabling long-range generalization.\n\nTo understand the rule of recurrent layers and their impact on the model’s generalization ability, this study varied the number of recurrent layers and tested the model’s generalization accuracy under different iterations. Fig. 5(a) shows the relative position relationship between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. The size and color depth of the non-sensor nodes indicate their distance from the nearest sensor nodes. Additionally, the data statistics in the upper right corner reveal the shortest path distribution between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. Notably, most non-sensor nodes are three steps away from sensor nodes, with the longest path being eleven steps. This information provides a crucial basis for subsequent analysis.\n\nImage: Fig 5\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (827KB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 5. Analysis of the number of the recurrent layers: (a) shortest path from sensor nodes to non-sensor nodes; (b) MAE distribution corresponding to different numbers of the recurrent layers; (c) MAPE corresponding to different numbers of the recurrent layers.\n\nExperimental results (Figs. 5(b) and 5(c)) indicate that the best performance is not achieved at the maximum iteration of 11, but at 3 iterations of recurrent layers. This finding demonstrates that more iterations are not necessarily better; instead, they need to match the majority of the shortest path between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. When the number of iterations exceeds a certain threshold (such as 1 in this case), further increasing the number of recurrent layers has limited improvement on the model’s prediction performance. This is primarily due to the attention mechanism’s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Thro"
  }

### aggregated_output

```text
PAGE 1 chars 2926 A leakage detection system extracting the most meaningful features with decision trees. Daniel Adanza Dopazo1 1 Stocker Rd, Exeter EX4 4PY 1d.adanza-dopazo @exeter.ac.uk ABSTRACT Introduction: Big quantities of water are wasted everyday due to leakage inside the water supply system of a fictitious L-Town. To learn from the experience and to unleash the creativity, an innovative solution has been provided where the main features about pressure, water flow and tank water levels have been extracted. The suggested approach implements decision trees for predicting the pressure values. Relying on their robustness against over fitting and the great accuracy of their predictions (Vinod & Suraj, 2018; Yarveicy & Ghiasi, 2017) The machine learning algorithm provides different predictions for each day and pressure sensor in the infrastructure. Ultimately, the obtained predictions are compared with the real pressure values, highlighting those cases where the real pressure in that area was meaningfully lower than what it should be in concordance with the rest of the data set. The suggested approach focuses on the prediction of the mean night pressure due to the stability of this attribute provoked by the low level of water consumption during nights. Data normalization: The initial data set containing samples of information for each five minutes is a great advantage since it is possible to distinguish all the changes among the water supply system within very short periods of time. However, it presents some pitfalls: Firstly, it is not computationally feasible to provide a reliable outcome with such a huge data set and secondly, you cannot see the forest through the trees with a reasonably big amount of pressure points and times. To tackle this problem, this approach includes some data normalization to extract the most meaningful features on daily bases, transforming the initial 5 minutes sample into the following attributes: • Minimum peak: Consists of the minimum daily registered value. • Maximum peak: Gathering the highest daily value • Mean daily: The average value without including the nights. (The time within 7 AM. and 8 PM.) • Mean morning: The average value for the period within 8 AM. and 12 AM. • Mean afternoon: The mean value for the time within 8 AM. and 12 AM. • Mean evening: The average value for the period within 12 AM. and 4 PM. • Mean night: The average value within 4 PM. and 9 PM. It is important to remark that the features have been extracted for each pressure, water flow and tank water level sensor. The suggested solution does not implement any process for outlier’s removal or resampling values, due to the nature of the problem it has been assumed that all values are completely reliable. The main idea underneath the normalization process consists of summarizing the most meaningful features spread around the different periods of the day. The underpinnings of the project:
PAGE 2 chars 3326 To have reliable predictions and accurate leakage detection, the suggested methodology relies on the following ideas: • Predicting the mean night pressure: due to the stability of this attribute it allows us to subtract the randomness provoked by the water household consumption and to be able to detect leakages uncomplicatedly. • Neighbor comparison: After the leakage detection has been performed, the results are compared with the geographically neighbor pressure sensors in order to compare their values and to detect leakages in a more reliable way. Methodology: After the initial data set has been normalized the suggested approach goes sequentially over the following steps: • Mean Night Pressure prediction: Firstly, the solution takes as inputs the normalized data set and implements the algorithm extra trees. The Extremely Randomized Trees Classifier is a type of ensemble learning which generates results based on a big number of de-correlated decision trees. Its inner functionality is similar to the random forest, only differing from it in the way that it constructs the trees. (Vinod & Suraj, 2018; Yarveicy & Ghiasi, 2017). The solution has been tested using one thousand trees in the forest. When making the predictions the algorithm outputs a different value for each pressure sensor and day implementing cross validation with ten folds (Porta, 2014). Cross validation is a statistical method for validating machine learning models. Consists of dividing the data set into folds and using only one of them as a test set and the rest of them as train set. This process is repeated iteratively until all folds are treated as test set separately. To generate unbiased results, the purposed solution constructs folds with the same number of sequential days. • Leakage detection: This second step takes as inputs the previously gathered predictions to establish comparisons within the real mean night pressure and the current predictions. If the generated predictions are higher than the real pressure values, it is possible to infer that is due to the inaccuracy in the predictions. If the predictions are about the same than the original pressure values, it is possible to assume that everything is ok. However, the algorithm highlights those cases when the predicted values where meaningfully lower than the real pressures. Whenever the algorithm detects an uncommonly low pressure in an area it flags it as a possible leakage. The established threshold for separating leakages and normal pressure values is -0.015. Ultimately, an adjacent pipe is manually selected based on the obtained leakage for the neighbor pressure sensor at the same specific time. The class attribute of the machine learning algorithm is the mean pressure during the night. The low water consumption in the night makes this attribute the steadiest and hence the most suitable for detecting anomalies in the pressure sensors. Results: After the data normalization has been performed, the machine learning algorithm makes predictions using cross validation with ten folds (Porta, 2014) making a different prediction for each day and pressure sensor. It is important to remark that ten different folds have been created for each class attribute to predict. Under this scenario, the machine learning algorithm has proven to be very robust
PAGE 3 chars 1255 obtaining an average of 0.0235% of relative error rate within all pressure sensors. The breakdown results for each single pressure sensor have been gathered in Figure 1. Where the blue line represents the relative error rate in percentage and the red line consists of the residual error. Figure 1: Breakdown results for the accuracy of the algorithm when predicting the mean night pressure. The prediction results have been compared with the real mean pressure values to highlight those cases where the pressure was lower than expected. As a result of that, 109 different leakages have been detected coming from 31 different pressure sensors. The breakdown results for leakage detection have been gathered in Figure 2, showing in the number of detected leakages in each pressure sensor. Figure 2: Breakdown results for leakage detection. Conclusions: In conclusion, we can say that is possible to provide an approach based on random forest regression able to detect leakages based on the anomalies found by the pressure sensors through the entire water supply system. The machine learning algorithm has proven to work in a robust way with barely a relative error rate of 0.0235%. Leaving a very strong position for performing leakage detection afterwards.
PAGE 4 chars 1983 Keywords: Leakage, pressure, ALC, water supply system, water flow. References: Porta, M. (2014). Cross-Validation (6th ed.). Oxford University Press. Vinod, N., & Suraj, H. (2018). Performance evaluation of bearing degradation based on stationary wavelet decomposition and extra trees regression. World Journal of Engineering, 15(5), 646– 658. https://doi.org/10.1108/WJE-12-2017-0403 Yarveicy, H., & Ghiasi, M. M. (2017). Modeling of gas hydrate phase equilibria: Extremely randomized trees and LSSVM approaches. Journal of Molecular Liquids, 243, 533–541. https://doi.org/https://doi.org/10.1016/j.molliq.2017.08.053 SUMMARY To learn from experience and to unleash the creativity, a novelty approach has been proposed with the main aim of detecting the different leakages of a fictitious L-town, containing a total of thirty three different pressure sensors in the whole infrastructure. To tackle this problem, a new solution has been presented based on the prediction of the mean night pressure for each pressure sensor located in the infrastructure. After the predictions have been generated, the solution compares them with the real values to highlight those cases when the pressure was meaningfully lower than expected, being able to detect leakages in the infrastructure in a reliable way. The machine learning algorithm has proven to be very accurate with barely 0.0235% of relative error rate, making a very reliable base for performing leakage detection. Based on those predictions a total of 109 different leakages have been found distributed inside 31 different pressure sensors. As a main conclusion we can infer that it is possible to build a leakage detection system based on the anomalies found in the pressure during nights, with the help of the stability of this attribute and the great accuracy of the machine learning algorithm the solution was able to make predictions with barely 0.0235% of relative error rate and being able to identify 102 different leakages.

Modeling of gas hydrate phase equilibria: Extremely randomized trees and LSSVM approaches. Journal of Molecular Liquids, 243, 533–541. https://doi.org/https://doi.org/10.1016/j.molliq.2017.08.053 SUMMARY To learn from experience and to unleash the creativity, a novelty approach has been proposed with the main aim of detecting the different leakages of a fictitious L-town, containing a total of thirty three different pressure sensors in the whole infrastructure. To tackle this problem, a new solution has been presented based on the prediction of the mean night pressure for each pressure sensor located in the infrastructure. After the predictions have been generated, the solution compares them with the real values to highlight those cases when the pressure was meaningfully lower than expected, being able to detect leakages in the infrastructure in a reliable way. The machine learning algorithm has proven to be very accurate with barely 0.0235% of relative error rate, making a very reliable base for performing leakage detection. Based on those predictions a total of 109 different leakages have been found distributed inside 31 different pressure sensors. As a main conclusion we can infer that it is possible to build a leakage detection system based on the anomalies found in the pressure during nights, with the help of the stability of this attribute and the great accuracy of the machine learning algorithm the solution was able to make predictions with barely 0.0235% of relative error rate and being able to identify 102 different leakages.\\r\\n\"}"
